Assume that the conditions in Theorem
5.3
are satisfied. When
C_{b}>\sqrt{\frac{10}{D_{4}\widetilde{p}}}\vee\frac{h}{2}\sqrt{6C_{a}}
with
D_{4}=\frac{\lambda^{*2}\widetilde{p}^{2}}{512d^{2}r^{4}\sigma^{2}}
, the following tail inequality holds for all
t>|{\mathcal{T}}_{0}^{RL}|
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{k\in\mathcal{K}}|(\widehat{\beta}^{RL}_{k}(\mathcal{I}_{k,t})-\beta_{k})^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{z}|\geq\frac{\epsilon_{t+1}^{RL}}{2}\right)\leq 15KT^{-4}.

Proposition
5.1
demonstrates that the initial estimator derived from randomly collected samples maintains the critical safety margin of
h/4
with high probability, which ensures that superior arms are included in the candidate set
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{\bm{x}_{t}}
.
Proposition
5.2
establishes a uniform convergence bound for the corrupted all-sample estimator. The resulting convergence rate explicitly depends on the order of
C
, with the bound reducing to the uncorrupted case when
C=0
.

The convergence guarantees (Propositions
5.1
-
5.2
) ensure arm selections are made using statistically valid comparisons, yielding the fairness guarantee through: (i) initial safe exploration, and (ii) controlled adaptive estimation errors. Based on these convergence guarantees, the following theorem establishes that, under the corruption model (
5.1
), Algorithm
3
still admits
(1-\frac{1}{T})
-fairness. This demonstrates that the algorithm completely withstands adversarial corruption at the fairness level.

Theorem 5.3
.

Suppose that Assumptions in Theorem
3.1
hold. When
T>\sqrt{15K}
, the policy defined by Algorithm
3
satisfies
(1-\frac{1}{T})
-fairness.

Having established fairness under corruption, we now derive the cumulative regret upper bound for Algorithm
3
, quantifying the performance cost of simultaneously maintaining fairness and robustness.

Theorem 5.4

(Upper bounds for Robust Linear Contextual Bandit)
.

Assume that the conditions in Theorem
5.3
are satisfied. Then the cumulative regret triggered by Algorithm
3
is bounded by

R_{T}=O(\log^{2}T+C).

Theorem
5.4
provides upper bounds with additive corruption dependence for
fair
linear contextual bandits. We further establish an algorithm-independent minimax lower bound for all fair admissible policies under corruption budget
C
, confirming the necessity of the linear
C
-dependence term in Theorem
5.5
. Comparing the lower bound with the upper bound in Theorem
5.4
, we can conclude that the policy defined by Algorithm
3
is rate-optimal under corruption budget
C
in the minimax sense.

Theorem 5.5

(Lower bounds for Linear Contextual Bandit Under Corruption)
.

Let
\Pi
denote all admissible policies that admits
(1-\delta)
-fairness with
\delta=\Theta(1/T)
. For the problem class
\mathcal{P}
satisfies Assumptions
1
-
3
, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\pi\in\Pi}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}R_{T}(\pi)=\Omega\left(\log^{2}T+C\right).

5.2.2
Smooth Reward Function

For the smooth contextual bandit problem under adversarial corruption, recall that the observed reward at time
t
is as in (
4.1
) with corruption
c_{t}
satisfying (
4.2
). We define the corrupted local polynomial estimator as

\displaystyle\widehat{f}^{RS}_{q-1,k}(\bm{x})=\widehat{f}^{\mathrm{LP}}\left(g(\bm{x});\mathcal{T}_{q-1,k}^{RS},H_{q-1,k}^{RS},\mathfrak{b}(\beta)\right),

(5.3)

where the corrupted samples collected at epoch
q
for arm
k
are given by
\mathcal{T}_{q,k}^{RS}=\left\{\left(\bm{x}_{t},\widetilde{y}_{t}\right):t\in\mathcal{T}_{q}^{RS},\,\pi_{t}=k\right\}
.
We modify two critical events that characterize whether epoch
q
remains well‑controlled under adversarial corruption as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{RS}_{q}=\left\{\min_{k\in\mathcal{K}}N_{q,k}^{RS}\geq\left(\frac{(C^{\frac{2\beta^{\prime}}{2\beta^{\prime}-1}}\vee 4^{q})\log(T\delta_{A}^{-d})}{C_{K}}\right)^{\frac{2\beta+d}{2\beta}}\left(\log T\right)^{(2\beta+d)\frac{2\beta-\beta^{\prime}+1}{2\beta(\beta^{\prime}-1)}}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{RS}_{q}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{(i) }\text{ for all }k\in\mathcal{K},S_{q,k}^{RS}\text{ is weakly }(\frac{c_{0}}{2^{d}},H_{q,k}^{RS})\text{-regular at all }\bm{x}\in S_{q,k}^{RS}\cap G\\
&\text{(ii) }\left|\widehat{f}_{q,k}^{RS}(\bm{x})-f^{*}_{k}(\bm{x})\right|\leq\epsilon_{q}^{RS}/2\text{ for all }\bm{x}\in S_{q,k}^{RS}\text{ and }k\in\mathcal{K}\end{aligned}\right\},

with
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{q}^{RS}=\bigcap_{1\leq j\leq q}\mathcal{G}_{q}^{RS}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{RS}=\bigcap_{1\leq j\leq q}\mathcal{M}_{q}^{RS}.
In particular, maintaining these events under adversarial perturbations is highly challenging, because attacks can easily fragment the sample support and expand the “inestimable regions”, further ruining estimations. To address this problem, our analysis tightly couples the smoothness structure of the reward functions with the corruption‑scaled designs to defend the adversary’s interventions. The following propositions formalize this robustness guarantee.

Proposition 5.6
.

Suppose Assumptions
4
-
8
hold. Under event
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{q-1}^{RS}\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{RS}
, for sufficiently large
T
,
S_{q,k}^{RS}
is weakly
(\frac{c_{0}}{2^{d}},H_{q,k}^{RS})
-regular at all
\bm{x}\in S_{q,k}^{RS}\cap G
for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

When
T
is sufficiently large and
Q^{RS}>1
, under Assumptions
4
-
8
, for any
1\leq q\leq Q^{RS}-1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left((\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{q}^{RS})^{C}\cup(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{RS})^{C}\right)\leq\frac{K\left(5+2M_{\beta}^{2}\right)q}{T}.

(5.4)

The analytical challenge stems from a fundamental tension: while extending epochs dilutes the influence of each corrupted sample, it simultaneously shrinks the estimation radius. This shrinkage imposes a stricter requirement on the regularity of sample support, but adversarial corruption even makes it theoretically harder to control and analyze. Proposition
5.6
bridges this gap by reconstructing the probabilistic and geometric reasoning needed to preserve weak regularity under corruption. It shows that even when epochs are lengthened to counteract corruption, the sample support can retain its regularity provided the local polynomial estimation error from the preceding epoch remains controlled by our designed threshold. This result actively defends regularity against the distortions introduced by adversarial perturbations. Proposition
5.7
then guarantees with high probability that these “good events” persist throughout learning.

Together, Propositions
5.6
and
5.7
serve the base for Theorems
5.8
and
5.9
, that is, even under adversarial perturbations, fairness is preserved and our algorithm can still achieve sub-linear regret bounds. Note that if the algorithm only performs a single epoch of random exploration (i.e.,
Q^{RS}=1
), fairness trivially holds because all arms are selected with equal probability, precluding any estimated merit-based bias.

Theorem 5.8
.

Suppose Assumptions
4
-
8
hold. For sufficiently large
T
, the policy defined by Algorithm
4
satisfies (
1-\delta
)-fairness with
\delta=\widetilde{O}(1/T)
.

Theorem 5.9

(Upper bounds for Robust Smooth Contextual Bandit)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4
-
8
hold and
Q^{RS}>1
. The expected cumulative regret admits the following upper bounds:

\displaystyle R_{T}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{O}(T^{1-\frac{(1+\alpha)\beta}{2\beta+d}}+C^{1+\alpha}T^{1-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+d}(1+\alpha)}),\quad\quad&\alpha\beta\leq\frac{d}{2};\\
\widetilde{O}(T^{1-\frac{(1+\alpha)\beta}{2\beta+d}}+C^{\frac{2\beta+d}{2\beta}+[\frac{d}{2\beta}-\alpha]\frac{1}{2\beta^{\prime}-1}}),\quad\quad&\alpha\beta>\frac{d}{2}.\end{cases}

Theorem
5.8
establishes that our robust smooth bandit algorithm is attack-resistant, which guarantees uniform fairness with probability at least
1-\widetilde{O}(1/T)
. Moreover, Theorem
5.9
establishes the
first
regret upper bound for fair smooth contextual bandits under adversarial attacks, exhibiting a multiplicative dependence on the corruption budget
C
. When
\alpha\beta\leq\frac{d}{2}
, while the bound contains a
C^{1+\alpha}
-scaling term
T^{1-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+d}(1+\alpha)}
, its impact is inherently limited due to the smoothness of reward functions. Even in the worst case when
\alpha=0
, this term simplifies to
T^{d/(2\beta+d)}
, which becomes negligible as the smoothness parameter
\beta
grows large. The upper bound crucially diminishes sharper margin conditions (large
\alpha
) and smoother reward functions (large
\beta
). Notably, when
C=O(T^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}})
, the dominant term becomes the uncorrupted regret
\widetilde{O}(T^{1-\frac{(1+\alpha)\beta}{2\beta+d}})
, highlighting that for moderate corruption, the effect of adversarial perturbations can be effectively neutralized by the smoothness of the reward functions.
When
\alpha\beta>\frac{d}{2}
, the amplification effect of
C
on
T
is mitigated by the improved margin condition, yet the exponent of
C
remains greater than 1. Specifically, the exponent is bounded by
\frac{2\beta+d}{2\beta}
, demonstrating that smoother reward functions (larger
\beta
) effectively constrain the adversarial impact within a manageable range.

To fully characterize the fundamental limits of achieving robustness alongside fairness, we establish the minimax lower bounds for smooth contextual bandits. This analysis addresses a significant gap in the literature: while
C
-budgeted corrupted lower bounds for smooth functions have been explored in the framework of Bayesian Optimization
(
13
)
, their characterization in the contextual bandit setting, especially under the constraints of meritocratic fairness, remains largely unexplored. We develop novel analytical techniques to construct adversarial instances that simultaneously satisfy fairness requirements while maximizing estimation difficulty.

Theorem 5.10

(Lower bounds for Smooth Contextual Bandit Under Corruption)
.

Fix positive parameters
\alpha
,
\beta
with
\alpha\beta<d
. Let
\Pi
denote all admissible policies that admits
(1-\widetilde{O}(\frac{1}{T}))
-fairness. For the problem class
\mathcal{P}
satisfying Assumptions
4
-
8
, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\pi\in\Pi}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}R_{T}(\pi)=\Omega\left(T^{\frac{\beta+d-\alpha\beta}{2\beta+d}}+C^{\frac{\alpha\beta+\beta}{\beta+d}}T^{\frac{d-\alpha\beta}{\beta+d}}\right).

In Theorem
5.10
, our lower bounds reveal a fundamental shift in the cost of robustness. Unlike linear bandits, where corruption typically adds a separate
O(C)
term to the regret, we prove that for smooth functions,
C
and
T
are inevitably coupled. This finding answers a key question: the standard additive regret is theoretically impossible. In smooth, fair settings, the adversary’s budget has an amplified impact: the same level of corruption
C
inflicts significantly greater long-term regret compared to simpler environments like linear rewards, as its per-unit harm scales with
T
.

In the regime
\alpha\beta\leq d/2
where performance is governed by the problem’s intrinsic complexity rather than a strong margin condition, the minimax-optimality guarantee holds when the corruption budget satisfies
C=O(T^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}})
. While a corresponding threshold exists for the margin-dominant regime (
\alpha\beta>d/2
), we focus on the former as it is independent of external margin conditions. Notably, in both regimes, beyond optimality thresholds, the gap vanishes as the smoothness parameter
\beta\rightarrow\infty
, demonstrating that stronger smoothness mitigates the impact of corruption on regret.

6
Numerical Experiments

In this section, we present a series of numerical experiments to evaluate the practical performance of our proposed fair and robust contextual bandit algorithms against other benchmarks. First, we compare our fair algorithms against standard baselines under typical stochastic conditions (Section
6.1
). Second, we examine how these algorithms defend adversarial reward manipulations (Section
6.2
). Finally, we apply our algorithms to a real-world wine brokerage scenario to assess its performance in practical applications (Section
6.3
). Detailed configuration of all the experiments and more experimental results can be found in the supplement.

6.1
Verifying Fairness and Regret in the Stochastic Setting

The primary objectives of this experiment are twofold: First, to validate that the proposed algorithm achieves regret comparable to existing minimax-optimal contextual bandit methods; Second, to demonstrate its effectiveness in reducing unfair decisions while maintaining competitive performance. To quantify fairness in practice, we record the cumulative count of unfair decisions
\mathcal{U}_{t}
, providing a direct measure of system performance over the horizon.

6.1.1
Linear Setting

We compare the proposed Fair OLS algorithm with several existing methods: (1) a standard greedy algorithm
(
7
)
, (2) a UCB-style algorithm
(
1
)
, (3) OLS bandit algorithm
(
27
)
, and (4) a random baseline for benchmarking, which is perfectly fair but does not learn.

Consider a linear contextual bandit problem with
K=10
arms and context dimension
d=10
. Contexts are drawn uniformly from
\mathcal{X}=[-1,1]^{d}
at each round. The true reward function for arm
k
given context
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
follows a linear model with arm-specific structure:

y_{k}(\bm{x})=f^{*}_{k}(\bm{x})+\varepsilon=w_{k}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{x}+b_{k}+\varepsilon,

where
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
with
\sigma=0.05
represents stochastic noise. The weight vectors
w_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are constructed to create local advantage patterns.
Specifically, the weight matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times d}
is defined cyclically:
W_{k,j}=2\cdot\mathbb{I}(j\equiv k)+1\cdot\mathbb{I}(j\equiv k+1)-1\cdot\mathbb{I}(j\equiv k-1),
where the equivalence
\equiv
is taken modulo
d
. Therefore, each arm is strongest in its corresponding dimension while being directly influenced by its immediate cyclical neighbors.
The bias terms
b_{k}=0.5\sin(2\pi k/K)
introduce variations across arms.

(a)

Linear Setting

(b)

Smooth Setting

Figure 2:
Performance comparison of linear and smooth contextual bandit algorithms in the stochastic setting. Left: Cumulative regret over time. Right: Cumulative unfair decisions over time.

Note: Lines show mean values from 10 independent runs, with shaded areas representing 95% confidence intervals.

We conducted 10 independent runs with a time horizon of
T=5000
. Results are presented in Figure
2
(a) with mean values accompanied by 95% confidence intervals. From the left regret comparison plot in Figure
2
(a), we observe that our Fair OLS algorithm achieves a cumulative regret comparable to other minimax-optimal baselines. This empirically validates that the proposed fairness mechanism does not lead to a significant degradation in learning efficiency, despite the theoretical introduction of an additional logarithmic factor. More importantly, the right plot tracking the cumulative number of unfair decisions reveals a stark contrast: our algorithm effectively eliminates unfair decisions over time, while the benchmark algorithms, despite varying in severity, consistently exhibit substantial unfairness throughout the learning process.

6.1.2
Smooth Setting

To evaluate performance in the non-parametric setting, we compare our smooth fair algorithm with the original smooth bandit baseline
(
31
)
. Since the exact implementation of inestimable-region detection places a heavy computational burden, we directly follow the numerical experiment of
31
and adopt their simplified smooth bandit algorithm for empirical comparison, which is a practical and computationally efficient UCB-style variant shown to achieve strong empirical performance. As a baseline, we also include a random policy.

We consider a contextual bandit problem with
K=4
arms and context dimension
d=2
. Contexts are sampled uniformly from the space
\mathcal{X}=[-1,1]^{d}
in each round. The true reward function for arm
k
given context
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
is defined as:

y_{k}(\bm{x})=f_{k}^{*}(\bm{x})+\varepsilon=\exp\left(-\|\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}\right)+\varepsilon,

where
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
with
\sigma=0.05
represents stochastic noise, and the set of arm centers is
\bm{\mu}_{k}\in\{(0.5,0.5),(-0.5,0.5),(-0.5,-0.5),(0.5,-0.5)\}
for
k=0,1,2,3
. The reward structure introduces sufficient complexity to challenge the learning algorithms while satisfying the smoothness requirements (
\beta
-Hölder continuity) necessary for our theoretical analysis. The smoothness parameter is set to
\beta=5
.

Consistent with the linear setting, we perform 10 independent runs with a time horizon of
T=5000
. Results are presented in Figure
2
(b). The regret plot shows our fair smooth algorithm maintains a competitive performance, while the cumulative unfairness plot confirms it substantially reduces unfair decisions.
Note that to demonstrate algorithmic efficacy within a finite horizon
T
, we apply a simplified version of the epoch schedule (detailed in the supplement), which modifies only the constant factors while preserving the original asymptotic order; this may result in a small performance gap relative to the theoretical bound. The same simplified schedule is employed in subsequent experiments.

6.2
Robustness Under Adversarial Reward Perturbations

6.2.1
Adversarial Linear Setting

In this experiment, we assess the robustness of the proposed algorithms against adversarial observations, which allows an adversary to tamper with the observed rewards of specific arms using a finite total budget
C
. We use the same linear contextual bandit instance as in Section
6.1.1
(
K=10
,
d=10
). The adversarial mechanism is implemented such that five arms out of ten, designated as the vulnerable arms, are susceptible to attack. The adversary is granted a budget
C=200
to execute attacks by manipulating the expected reward for a vulnerable arm
k
to a misleading low value
-4
. The manipulation only succeeds if the remaining budget is sufficient. We perform 10 independent runs with a time horizon
T=10,000
. The results are summarized in Figure
3
(a).

(a)

Linear Setting

(b)

Smooth Setting

Figure 3:
Performance comparison of linear and smooth contextual bandit algorithms in the adversarial setting. Left: Cumulative regret over time. Right: Cumulative unfair decisions over time.

Note: Lines show mean values from 10 independent runs, with shaded areas representing 95% confidence intervals.

The experimental outcomes highlight the performance divergence between robust and non-robust policies under adversarial influence. Specifically, the non-robust algorithms exhibit a near-linear and rapid growth trend in both cumulative regret and cumulative unfair decisions throughout the time horizon. Notably, the Fair OLS algorithm performs, under attack, nearly as poorly as those that are inherently unfair. In contrast, our proposed Robust Fair OLS algorithm demonstrates strong resilience against the attack. Its cumulative regret quickly transitions to a slow growth rate, indicating effective mitigation of the reward corruption. Furthermore, its cumulative unfairness count grows at a markedly slower rate than its competitors, confirming that the fairness mechanism successfully operates even when the observed reward is attacked.

6.2.2
Adversarial Smooth Setting

In this experiment, we examine the impact of adversarial attacks in the complex non-parametric regime. We utilize the same underlying smooth contextual bandit problem as in Section
6.1.2
. The adversarial mechanism is configured similar to the linear setting: two arms out of four are targeted for corruption, with the attack attempting to push the expected reward to a misleading low value of
-0.1
, while constrained by the budget
C=200
. Following the same setup as previous experiments, we conduct 10 independent runs over a time horizon of
T=10,000
.

As shown in Figure
3
(b), for the fair smooth algorithm, we observe an approximately linear growth in both regret and unfair events, aligning with the theoretical analysis presented in Section
4
; the simplified smooth bandit algorithm also degrades in both regret and fairness under adversarial conditions.
In contrast, the robust fair smooth algorithm not only achieves the smallest regret trend but also effectively controls the number of unfair events. This validates the joint effectiveness and robustness of our proposed approach.

6.3
Real-World Validation

Dataset and Platform Design.
To further validate the practical applicability of our proposed robust fair algorithms, we conduct a real-world experiment using the Wine Quality Dataset from the UCI Machine Learning Repository
(
21
)
. This dataset comprises 6,497 wine samples, each described by 11 physicochemical features (e.g., acidity, sugar, pH, alcohol content) and a sensory quality rating (on a scale of 0–10) as the response variable. The feature set and realistic quality assessments provide a solid basis for simulating a wine brokerage platform. In our constructed scenario, a central system sequentially recommends wine agents (arms) to suppliers (users) based on the physicochemical features of each wine (contexts), aiming to maximize cumulative profit while ensuring fair exposure among agents. We consider three wine agents, each specializing in a distinct market segment: Agent 1 (Economy Agent) sells economic wines, Agent 2 (Mid-range Agent) sells mid-tier wines, and Agent 3 (Premium Agent) sells high-end wines. The context vector
\bm{x}_{t}
corresponds to the normalized physicochemical features of a wine sample at round
t
.

Reward Structure.
The reward functions are designed to reflect the intrinsic economic logic of each agent’s market niche: the premium agent profits most from high-quality wines, the economy agent from lower-quality wines, and the mid-range agent from wines of intermediate quality. Specifically, the reward functions are defined as follows:

\displaystyle y_{1}(q)=\frac{2}{1+\exp(-(q-6))},y_{3}(q)=\frac{2}{1+\exp((q-6))},y_{2}(q)=y_{1}(q)\times y_{3}(q).

These functions collectively create a structured competitive landscape where each arm is optimal in a distinct quality region, thereby presenting a meaningful test for merit-based fairness.

Feature Processing.

For linear contextual bandit algorithms, we use the original 11-dimensional features after standard normalization. However, smooth bandit algorithms rely on local polynomial regression, which is computationally expensive when the dimension is high due to the curse of dimensionality. To mitigate this, we first reduce the dimensionality to 3 using a neural network encoder. This encoder, which is a four-layer fully-connected network with ReLU activations, is trained as an encoder to preserve essential information from the original features. Thus, linear algorithms use the full 11-dimensional features, while smooth algorithms operate on the compressed 3-dimensional representations. To account for natural variations in the data, we run each algorithm over 10 random permutations of the wine dataset.

Fairness Threshold in Real Data.

Different with synthetic dataset, we adopt a practical fairness threshold: an unfair event is flagged only if a candidate arm’s observed reward is at least 0.01 lower than other arms for the given context. This tolerance, negligible relative to the reward scale, accommodates the natural variability in human expert scores, thus preventing measurement noise from being misattributed as algorithmic bias.

6.3.1
Benign Marketplace

In this experiment, we evaluate how well our algorithms balance fairness and efficiency in a stable, non-adversarial marketplace. As shown in the left panel of Figure
4
, the regret curves of our methods closely track those of the corresponding baselines, confirming that the fairness mechanisms do not cause extra profit loss for this platform, which is consistent with our theoretical expectations. More importantly, the right panel plots the cumulative unfair decisions: while the baseline algorithms accumulate a substantial number of unfair choices throughout the horizon, our fair variants sharply reduce such events. This reduction translates directly into a more equitable marketplace: wine agents are far less likely to receive undeservedly low exposure. By enforcing merit‑based exposure, our algorithms help maintain a healthy market environment where various kinds of wines reliably reach suitable agents, thereby supporting long‑term platform sustainability and agents trust.

Operationally, this means good agents are less likely to be buried. These findings show platform operators that fairness can coexist with profit, and can even sustain ecosystem vitality. When both suppliers and agents trust the matching process, they stay and invest, enriching platform diversity and long-term resilience.

(a)

Linear Setting

(b)

Smooth Setting

Figure 4:
Performance comparison of linear and smooth contextual bandit algorithms on the wine brokerage platform. Left: Cumulative regret over time. Right: Cumulative unfair decisions over time. Note: An unfair decision occurs when a candidate agent yields at least 0.01 lower observed profit than other agents to compensate for observation randomness. Lines show mean values from 10 independent runs, with shaded areas representing 95% confidence intervals.

6.3.2
Adversarial Marketplace

Beyond the benign setting, real-world platforms often operate in competitive environments. In a marketplace where multiple agents specialize in different quality segments, there exists an inherent incentive for participants to influence exposure outcomes. For instance, a mid‑range agent might benefit if both high‑end and economy offerings appear less attractive. Similarly, competing platforms may also seek to attack the brokerage system by systematically distorting reward signals. These scenarios reflect realistic threats.

To simulate this adversarial marketplace, we consider a scenario where agents attempt to monopolize certain market segments. Specifically, we model an attack that targets the high-end and economy agents: an adversary strategically corrupts their observed rewards to zero during the learning process, with the goal of making the mid‑range agent appear dominant across all quality segments. This mirrors real‑world attacks where a player might artificially suppress competitors’ performance metrics to gain unfair advantage. Our experiments examine whether the proposed robust fair algorithms can withstand such manipulation while maintaining both fairness and efficiency.

(a)

Linear Setting

(b)

Smooth Setting

Figure 5:
Performance comparison of linear and smooth contextual bandit algorithms on the wine brokerage platform under attack. Left: Cumulative regret over time. Right: Cumulative unfair decisions over time. Note: An unfair decision occurs when a candidate agent yields at least 0.01 lower observed profit than other agents to compensate for observation randomness. Lines show mean values from 10 independent runs, with shaded areas representing 95% confidence intervals.

The left panel of Figure
5
shows cumulative regret under attack for the linear and smooth algorithms, respectively. Only our robust fair algorithms maintain sub‑linear regret growth. In contrast, all other algorithms display near‑linear regret, with some exceeding even that of a random policy. The right panel tracks cumulative unfair decisions under the same attack. Under corruption, the unfairness curves of fair but non‑robust algorithms rise sharply, compared with the benign case. Our robust algorithms, however, keep unfairness close to the levels observed without attack. This pattern reveals a concrete dual failure in methods lacking robustness: they not only fail to learn efficiently (linear regret), but also systematically distort exposure (linear growth in unfairness). In this practical scenario, robustness mechanisms are necessary to preserve learning efficiency and exposure fairness when rewards are subject to manipulation.

7
Conclusions and Discussion

In this work, we develop the first framework for attack-resistant uniform fairness in contextual bandits, progressing from foundational algorithm design to the discovery of critical vulnerabilities and the establishment of robust governance. We started with proposing novel algorithms for both linear and smooth (non-parametric) reward settings, integrating arm elimination with confidence-bound chaining techniques to simultaneously guarantee
(1-\widetilde{O}(1/T))
-uniform fairness and near-optimal regret bounds. We then expose the fragility of fair systems by proving that a negligible
\widetilde{O}(1)
reward corruption can be strategically leveraged to induce persistent unfairness, which can either occur silently or lead to a total system collapse with linear regret. To address this, we design robust variants that protect the fairness guarantees
against adversarial attacks, and provide the first complete minimax analysis of regret under corruption. Notably, we achieve tight regret bounds of
O(\log^{2}T+C)
in linear settings. In the smooth regime, our algorithm retains near-minimax optimality for corruption levels up to
C=O(T^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}})
when external margin conditions are mild (i.e., in the general case
\alpha\beta\leq d/2
). A similarly structured optimality threshold dependent on the margin exponent
\alpha
also holds for the margin-dominant regime (
\alpha\beta>d/2
).

We conclude by highlighting limitations and future directions. First, while our smooth bandit algorithm requires
C=O(T^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}})
for minimax optimal regret, relaxing this constraint remains open. Second, extending our item-level fairness guarantees to hierarchical fairness constraints (e.g., group-item compositions) would enhance practical applicability. Third, our corruption model assumes static budgets; designing defenses against adaptive adversaries with strategic budget allocation warrants investigation. Finally, the computational complexity of non-parametric robust estimation motivates developing approximation techniques for large-scale deployment. These directions will bridge theoretical guarantees with real-world adversarial robustness requirements.

Supplemental Material

This appendix provides the complete technical details supporting the main paper. It is organized as follows: Section
A
contains proofs of the main theorems; Section
B
presents details in experiments.

Appendix A
Technical Proofs

This section provides the complete proofs of all main results, presented in the order they appear in the body of the paper.

A.1
Proofs in Section
3.1

We begin by stating two key propositions that support the subsequent analysis; their proofs are provided in Section
A.5
. Throughout this section, we define the event
\mathcal{A}=\{|\mathcal{I}_{k,0}|\geq\frac{1}{2K}|\mathcal{T}_{0}|\}
, event
\mathcal{B}=\{\max_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{k\in\mathcal{K}}|(\widehat{\beta}_{k}(\mathcal{I}_{k,0})-\beta_{k})^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{z}|<\frac{h}{4}\}
and event
\mathcal{W}_{t}=\{\max_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{k\in\mathcal{K}}|(\widehat{\beta}_{k}(\mathcal{I}_{k,t-1})-\beta_{k})^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{z}|<\frac{\epsilon_{t}}{2}\}
. For brevity, throughout the analysis, we say that fairness is fulfilled at time
t
, with the understanding that at
t
, (
2.2
) holds for all context
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
and all pair of arms
i,j\in\mathcal{K}
.

Proposition A.1
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
-
3
hold and assume
T>2d
. When
C_{a}>\frac{20K^{2}}{\widetilde{p}D_{2}}\vee\frac{8K^{2}}{\widetilde{p}^{2}}\vee\frac{640K}{h^{2}D_{1}}
with
D_{1}=\frac{\lambda^{*2}\widetilde{p}^{2}}{32d^{2}r^{4}\sigma^{2}K^{2}}
,
D_{2}=\min\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{\lambda^{*}}{8r^{2}}\right)
, the following tail inequality holds:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\max_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{k\in\mathcal{K}}|(\widehat{\beta}_{k}(\mathcal{I}_{k,0})-\beta_{k})^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{z}|\geq\frac{h}{4})\leq 4KT^{-4}.

Proposition A.2
.

Assume that the conditions in Proposition
A.1
are satisfied. Furthermore, when
C_{b}>\sqrt{\frac{10}{D_{4}\widetilde{p}}}\vee\frac{h}{2}\sqrt{2C_{a}+1}
with
D_{4}=\frac{\lambda^{*2}\widetilde{p}^{2}}{512d^{2}r^{4}\sigma^{2}}
, the following tail inequality holds for all
t>|{\mathcal{T}}_{0}|
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\max_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{k\in\mathcal{K}}|(\widehat{\beta}_{k}(\mathcal{I}_{k,t})-\beta_{k})^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{z}|\geq\frac{\epsilon_{t+1}}{2})\leq 8KT^{-4}.

A.1.1
Proof of Theorem
3.1

When
t\leq|\mathcal{T}_{0}|
, the algorithm randomly pull arms with equal probability. Thus, it is clear that for any
i,j\in\mathcal{K}
, and all
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=i|\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\bm{x}_{t}=\bm{x})=\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=j|\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\bm{x}_{t}=\bm{x})
, which automatically fulfills fairness. Then we focus on the time that
t>|\mathcal{T}_{0}|
. At time
t
, for any
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\bm{x}_{t}=\bm{x}
, we consider two cases separately.

Case 1.

\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,0}\geq\max\limits_{l\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{k\}}\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{l,0}+h/2
.
The algorithm mandates that if
\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,0}\geq\max\limits_{l\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{k\}}\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{l,0}+h/2
, then
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=k|\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\bm{z}_{t}=\bm{z})=1
. This implies that fairness is fulfilled at time
t
when
\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}>\max_{l\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{k\}}\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{l}
. Denote
o:=\arg\max_{l\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{k\}}\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{l}
. Recall that
\mathcal{B}=\{\max_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{k\in\mathcal{K}}|(\widehat{\beta}_{k}(\mathcal{I}_{k,0})-\beta_{k})^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{z}|<\frac{h}{4}\}
. We proceed by showing that if event
\mathcal{B}
holds,

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{o}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,0}+\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,0}-\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{o,0}+\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{o,0}-\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{o}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle-\frac{h}{4}+\frac{h}{2}-\frac{h}{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

It implies that if event
\mathcal{B}
holds, fairness is fulfilled at time
t
. As we have established in Proposition
A.1
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B})\geq 1-4KT^{-4}
. Thus, at time
t
, fairness is fulfilled with probability at least
1-4KT^{-4}
under Case 1.

Case 2.

\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,0}\leq\max\limits_{l\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{k\}}\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{l,0}+h/2
. The algorithm pulls arm
\pi_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\bm{x})
uniformly at random. Therefore, for any
h\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{c}(\bm{x})
and any
g\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\bm{x})
,
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=h)=0<\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=g)
. It remains to show that
\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{g}>\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{h}
in order to fulfill fairness. Since
h\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{c}(\bm{x})
, we have
\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{g,t-1}>\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{h,t-1}+\epsilon_{t}
. Then under event
\mathcal{W}_{t}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{g}-\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{g}-\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{g,t-1}+\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{g,t-1}-\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{h,t-1}+\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{h,t-1}-\bm{z}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{h}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle-\frac{\epsilon_{t}}{2}+\epsilon_{t}-\frac{\epsilon_{t}}{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

It implies that if event
\mathcal{W}_{t}
holds, fairness is fulfilled at time
t
. As we have established in Proposition
A.2
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{W}_{t})\geq 1-8KT^{-4}
. Thus, at time
t
, fairness is fulfilled with probability at least
1-8KT^{-4}
.

Combining two cases, we can conclude that at time
t
, fairness is fulfilled with probability at least
1-8KT^{-4}
. Using union bound, the event that for all
t\in[T]
and all pair of arms
i,j\in\mathcal{K}
, fairness is fulfilled happens with probability at least
1-8KT^{-3}>1-1/T
as long as
T>2\sqrt{2K}
. This finishes the proof.
\square

A.1.2
Proof of Theorem
3.2

We decompose the cumulative regret into three parts:

\displaystyle R_{T}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left(\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{|\mathcal{T}_{0}|}\mathbb{E}\left(\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}}\right)+\sum_{t=|\mathcal{T}_{0}|+1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left((\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}})\mathbb{I}(\mathcal{W}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{t=|\mathcal{T}_{0}|+1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left((\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}})\mathbb{I}(\mathcal{W}_{t}^{C})\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3}.

First, since
\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}}=O(1)
, it is obvious that
R_{1}\lesssim\log T
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle R_{3}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=|\mathcal{T}_{0}|+1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left((\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}})\mathbb{I}(\mathcal{W}_{t}^{C})\right)\lesssim\sum_{t=|\mathcal{T}_{0}|+1}^{T}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{W}_{t}^{C})\leq T(8KT^{-4})=O(1).

It remains to bound
R_{2}
. If
\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,0}\geq\max\limits_{l\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{k\}}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{l,0}+h/2
, we have proved that
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=\arg\max_{l\in\mathcal{K}}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{l})\geq 1-4KT^{-4}
, which leads to

\mathbb{E}\left((\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}})\mathbb{I}(\mathcal{W}_{t})\mathbb{I}(\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,0}\geq\max\limits_{l\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{k\}}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{l,0}+h/2)\right)\lesssim 4KT^{-4}.

Otherwise, under event
\mathcal{W}_{t}
, for
o:=\arg\max_{l\in\mathcal{K}}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{l}
and for
v:=\arg\max_{l\in\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{\bm{x}_{t}}}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{l,t-1}
,

\displaystyle 0\geq\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{o,t-1}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{v,t-1}=

\displaystyle\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{o,t-1}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}{\beta}_{o}+\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}{\beta}_{o}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}{\beta}_{v}+\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}{\beta}_{v}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{v,t-1}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-\epsilon_{t}/2-\epsilon_{t}/2=-\epsilon_{t},

which implies that
\arg\max_{l\in\mathcal{K}}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{l}\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\bm{x}_{t})
. Then it follows that for any
k\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\bm{x}_{t})
,

\displaystyle\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{o,t-1}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,t-1}\leq(|\mathcal{K}_{c}(\bm{x}_{t})|-1)\epsilon_{t}\leq(K-1)\epsilon_{t},

which implies that

\displaystyle\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}{\beta}_{o}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}{\beta}_{k}=

\displaystyle\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}{\beta}_{o}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{o,t-1}+\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{o,t-1}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,t-1}+\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,t-1}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}{\beta}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon_{t}/2+(K-1)\epsilon_{t}+\epsilon_{t}/2

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K\epsilon_{t}.

Since
\pi_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\bm{x}_{t})
, it follows that
\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}{\beta}_{o}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}{\beta}_{\pi_{t}}\leq K\epsilon_{t}
under event
\mathcal{W}_{t}
, which leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left((\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}})\mathbb{I}(\mathcal{W}_{t})\mathbb{I}(\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{k,0}<\max\limits_{l\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{k\}}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\widehat{\beta}_{l,0}+h/2)\right)

\displaystyle\leq K\epsilon_{t}\mathbb{P}(0<(\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}})\leq K\epsilon_{t})

\displaystyle\lesssim K^{4}\epsilon_{t}^{2}\lesssim\log T/t,

where the second inequality is by taking union bound over all pair of arms on the margin condition (Assumption
2
).
In conclusion, under both cases,
\mathbb{E}\left((\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}})\mathbb{I}(\mathcal{W}_{t})\right)\lesssim\log T/t+T^{-4}
. Then we have

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=|\mathcal{T}_{0}|+1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left((\max_{k}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{k}-\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{\pi_{t}})\mathbb{I}(\mathcal{W}_{t})\right)\lesssim\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\log T/t+T^{-4})\lesssim\log^{2}T.

Combining the bounds on
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
and
R_{3}
, we have
R_{T}=O(\log^{2}T)
. This finishes the proof.
\square

A.1.3
Proof of Theorem
3.3

Lemma A.3
.

Consider the following two experiments: In the first, let
\theta_{i}\sim P_{i}
and
r_{i}^{1},\ldots,r_{i}^{t}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\theta_{i},\sigma^{2}\right)
, and
W
denote the joint distribution on
\left(\theta_{i},r_{i}^{1},\ldots,r_{i}^{t}\right)
. In the second, let
\theta_{i}\sim P_{i}
, and
r_{i}^{1},\ldots,r_{i}^{t}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\theta_{i},\sigma^{2}\right)
, and then re-draw the mean
\theta_{i}^{\prime}\sim P_{i}\left(r_{i}^{1},\ldots,r_{i}^{t}\right)
from its posterior distribution given the rewards. Let
\left(\theta_{i}^{\prime},r_{i}^{1},\ldots,r_{i}^{t}\right)\sim W^{\prime}
. Then,
W
and
W^{\prime}
are identical distributions.

Proof.

The proof follows exactly the same reasoning as Lemma 4 in
33
, replacing the Bernoulli likelihood with a Gaussian likelihood.
\square

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Let
\{x_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
be i.i.d. contexts at time
t
, each uniformly distributed on
\mathcal{X}=[-1,1]
. Consider the observations of arm 1 and 2:
Y_{t}^{(1)}=x_{t}+\epsilon_{t}
and
Y_{t}^{(2)}=\theta+\epsilon_{t}
, where
\theta\sim\mathrm{Unif}(-1/2,1/2)
and
\epsilon_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Step 1. Verifying the Assumptions.

We verify assumptions one by one. For Assumption
1
, the contexts
\{x_{t}:t=1,2,\ldots\}
are i.i.d. with density
1/2
with respect to Lebesgue measure, drawn from the fixed distribution
\mathbb{P}_{X}
with support
\mathcal{X}=[-1,1]\subseteq[-r,r]
by taking
r=1\geq 1
. Moreover,
\|\beta_{1}\|_{2}=\|(0,1)^{\mathrm{T}}\|_{2}=1
and
\|\beta_{2}\|_{2}=\|(\theta,0)^{\mathrm{T}}\|_{2}=|\theta|\leq 1/2<1
, so
\|\beta_{k}\|_{2}\leq b=1
holds for all
k\in\{1,2\}
. Thus, Assumption
1
is satisfied.

For Assumption
2
, consider the difference vectors: for
i=1,j=2
,
\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}=(-\theta,1)^{\mathrm{T}}
, so
\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})=x_{t}-\theta
; the case
i=2,j=1
yields
\theta-x_{t}=-(x_{t}-\theta)
, with the same absolute value. The probability
\mathbb{P}(0<|x_{t}-\theta|\leq\rho)
is required to satisfy the bound in Assumption
2
for all
\rho>0
. Since
x_{t}\sim\mathrm{Unif}[-1,1]
with density
1/2
and
|\theta|\leq 1/2
, for
\rho\leq 1/2
the interval
[\theta-\rho,\theta+\rho]\cap[-1,1]=[\theta-\rho,\theta+\rho]
, with Lebesgue measure
2\rho
. We obtain
\mathbb{P}(0<|x_{t}-\theta|\leq\rho)=(1/2)\cdot 2\rho=\rho
. For
\rho>1/2
, the probability is bounded by
1<2\rho
. Thus, taking
L=2>0
satisfies the condition for all
\rho>0
and all
i\neq j
. Assumption
2
therefore holds with
L=2
.

For Assumption
3
, take
h=1/4>0
. For arm 1,
Q_{1}=\{\bm{z}_{t}:\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{1}>\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{2}+h\}=\{x_{t}>\theta+h\}
; since
|\theta|\leq 1/2
,
\theta+h\leq 3/4<1
, so
\mathbb{P}(Q_{1})=[1-(\theta+h)]/2\geq(1/4)/2=1/8
. For arm 2,
Q_{2}=\{\bm{z}_{t}:\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{2}>\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\beta_{1}+h\}=\{x_{t}<\theta-h\}
;
\theta-h\geq-3/4>-1
, so
\mathbb{P}(Q_{2})=[(\theta-h)-(-1)]/2\geq(1/4)/2=1/8
. Thus,
\mathbb{P}(\bm{z}_{t}\in Q_{k})\geq\widetilde{p}=1/8
for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
. For the minimum eigenvalue condition, note that
\bm{z}_{t}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{t}\\
x_{t}&x_{t}^{2}\end{pmatrix},
so
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\mid Q_{k}\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\mu_{k}\\
\mu_{k}&\mathbb{E}\left[x_{t}^{2}\mid Q_{k}\right]\end{array}\right)
, where
\mu_{k}=\mathbb{E}[x_{t}\mid Q_{k}]
and
\mathbb{E}[x_{t}^{2}\mid Q_{k}]=\mathrm{Var}(x_{t}\mid Q_{k})+\mu_{k}^{2}
. It can be verified that
\min_{k}\lambda_{\min}(\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}_{t}\bm{z}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}\mid Q_{k}])\geq 0.002
, so taking
\lambda^{*}=0.002
satisfies the condition. Assumption
3
therefore holds with
h=1/4
,
\widetilde{p}=1/8
, and
\lambda^{*}=0.002
.

Step 2. Establishing the Concept of
\delta^{\prime}
-Distinguishability and Its Consequences.

We analyze the behavior at time
t+1
based on
t
prior interactions. For simplicity, we suppress the dependency on
t
in some of our notations. Assume the observations are generated from
\theta_{0}
. We say the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}

\delta^{\prime}
-distinguishes at
x\in\mathcal{X}
if either

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}}}[\theta^{\prime}>x]\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}

(A.1)

or

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}}}[\theta^{\prime}<x]\geq 1-\delta^{\prime},

(A.2)

where
\mathbb{P}_{\theta\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}}
denotes the posterior distribution of
\theta
given
\mathcal{F}_{t}
.

Let
s
denote the number of observations from arm 2 up to time
t
, and let
\bar{Y}
be the sample mean of these
s
observations. We first establish a lower bound on
\mathbb{P}(s\geq t/16)
. For any
(1-\delta)
-fair algorithm, it holds with probability at least
1-\delta
over
\mathcal{F}_{T}|\theta_{0}
that, for each
t\in[T]
,
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=2\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\theta_{0}>x_{t})\geq 1/2
. Since
\mathbb{P}(\theta_{0}>x_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1})\geq\mathbb{P}(-1/2>x_{t})=1/4
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=2\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1})\geq(1/2)\times(1/4)=1/8.

Let
\mathcal{E}
denote the global fairness event described above. Define
p_{t}:=\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=2\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\mathcal{E})\geq 1/8
. Then for any
\theta_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}|\theta_{0}}\left(s<\frac{t}{16}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(s<\frac{t}{16}\;\middle|\;\mathcal{E}\right)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})+\mathbb{P}\left(s<\frac{t}{16}\;\middle|\;\mathcal{E}^{c}\right)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(s<\frac{t}{16}\;\middle|\;\mathcal{E}\right)+\delta.

(A.3)

Since
\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{I}\{\pi_{t}=2\}-p_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\mathcal{E}]=0
, the sequence
\{M_{i}:=\mathbb{I}\{\pi_{i}=2\}-p_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
forms a martingale difference sequence with respect to
\{\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\mathcal{E}\}_{i\geq 1}
. Note that
\sum_{i=1}^{t}M_{i}=s-\sum_{i=1}^{t}p_{i}
. Given
|M_{i}|\leq 1
, Azuma-Hoeffding’s inequality yields

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{t}M_{i}\leq-\lambda\;\middle|\;\mathcal{E}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2\lambda^{2}}{t}\right),\quad\lambda>0.

As
\sum_{i=1}^{t}p_{i}\geq t/8
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(s<\frac{t}{16}\;\middle|\;\mathcal{E}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(s-\sum_{i=1}^{t}p_{i}<\frac{t}{16}-\sum_{i=1}^{t}p_{i}\;\middle|\;\mathcal{E}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(s-\sum_{i=1}^{t}p_{i}<-\frac{t}{16}\;\middle|\;\mathcal{E}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{t}{128}\right).

By (
A.1.3
), this implies for the fixed
\theta_{0}
:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}|\theta_{0}}\left(s<\frac{t}{16}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{t}{128}\right)+\delta,

and hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}|\theta_{0}}\left(s\geq\frac{t}{16}\right)\geq 1-\delta-\exp\left(-\frac{t}{128}\right).

(A.4)

Now suppose (
A.1
) holds. Since the posterior distribution
\theta^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}}
is a Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(\bar{Y},1/s)
truncated to the interval
[-1/2,1/2]
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi\left(\sqrt{s}(1/2-\bar{Y})\right)-\Phi\left(\sqrt{s}(x-\bar{Y})\right)}{\Phi\left(\sqrt{s}(1/2-\bar{Y})\right)-\Phi\left(\sqrt{s}(-1/2-\bar{Y})\right)}\geq 1-\delta^{\prime},

which rearranges to

\displaystyle\Phi(z_{x})\leq(1-\delta^{\prime})\Phi(a)+\delta^{\prime}\Phi(b),

(A.5)

where
\Phi
is the cumulative distribution function of a standard normal distribution,
a:=\sqrt{s}(-1/2-\bar{Y})
,
b:=\sqrt{s}(1/2-\bar{Y})
, and
z_{x}:=\sqrt{s}(x-\bar{Y})
.

We next show that if
\delta^{\prime}<1/2
and
a\leq-\sqrt{2\log\frac{1-\delta^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}}
, then
\Phi(a)\leq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{1-\delta^{\prime}}
. Since
\delta^{\prime}<1/2
, we have
\frac{1-\delta^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}>1
, so
\log\frac{1-\delta^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}>0
and
-\sqrt{2\log\frac{1-\delta^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}}<0
. By the symmetry of the standard normal distribution,

\Phi(a)=\mathbb{P}(Z\leq a)=\mathbb{P}(Z\geq-a).

Note that
\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{1-\delta^{\prime}}<1
. Thus,

-a\geq\sqrt{2\log\frac{1-\delta^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}}\implies\frac{a^{2}}{2}\geq\log\frac{1-\delta^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}\implies e^{-a^{2}/2}\leq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{1-\delta^{\prime}}.

Hoeffding’s tail bound for the standard normal gives
\mathbb{P}(Z\geq-a)\leq e^{-a^{2}/2}
, so
\Phi(a)\leq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{1-\delta^{\prime}}
. Combining this with (
A.5
), we obtain
\Phi(z_{x})\leq(1-\delta^{\prime})\Phi(a)+\delta^{\prime}\Phi(b)\leq 2\delta^{\prime}
whenever
\delta^{\prime}<1/2
and
a\leq-\sqrt{2\log\frac{1-\delta^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}}
. Since
\delta^{\prime}
can be arbitrarily small as discussed later, we only consider the condition
a\leq-\sqrt{2\log\frac{1-\delta^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}}
here.

Define the event
\mathcal{A}:=\{|\bar{Y}-\theta_{0}|\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{s}\log\frac{1}{4\delta^{\prime}}}\}
. Given a fixed
\theta_{0}
, the observations from arm 2 are i.i.d. with mean
\theta_{0}
.
For any fixed set of time indices where arm 2 was pulled, the rewards at those times are i.i.d. with mean
\theta_{0}
, regardless of how those time indices were selected.
Then it follows that for any fixed
\theta_{0}
, by Hoeffding’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}|\theta}(\mathcal{A}\mid s\geq t/16)\geq 1-2\sqrt{4\delta^{\prime}}.

(A.6)

Under event
\mathcal{A}
and the conditions
s\geq t/16
,
|\theta_{0}|\leq 1/4
,
t>8192\log(1/\delta^{\prime})
, and
\delta^{\prime}<1/2
, we have

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\sqrt{s}\left(-\frac{1}{2}-\bar{Y}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{s}\left(-\frac{1}{2}-\theta_{0}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{s}\log\frac{1}{4\delta^{\prime}}}\right)\leq\sqrt{s}\left(-\frac{1}{4}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{s}\log\frac{1}{4\delta^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{s}+\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{4\delta^{\prime}}}\leq-\sqrt{2\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}}\leq-\sqrt{2\log\frac{1-\delta^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}}.

This implies
\Phi(z_{x})\leq 2\delta^{\prime}
, so
z_{x}\leq-\sqrt{2\log\frac{1}{4\delta^{\prime}}}
. By the definition of
z_{x}
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{s}\left(x-\theta_{0}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{s}\log\frac{1}{4\delta^{\prime}}}\right)\leq-\sqrt{2\log\frac{1}{4\delta^{\prime}}}\implies x-\theta_{0}\leq-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{s}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}\leq-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}.

A symmetric argument under
\mathcal{A}
and the same conditions shows that (
A.2
) implies

\displaystyle x-\theta_{0}\geq\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}.

Therefore, under
\mathcal{A}
and the conditions
s\geq t/16
,
|\theta_{0}|\leq 1/4
,
t>8192\log(1/\delta^{\prime})
, and
\delta^{\prime}<1/2
,
\mathcal{F}_{t}

\delta^{\prime}
-distinguishes at
x
implies either
x-\theta_{0}\leq-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}
or
x-\theta_{0}\geq\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}.

Step 3. Uncertainty Constraints for Fair Algorithms.
We say
\mathcal{F}_{t}
is unfair for
(\theta_{0},x)
if either
\theta_{0}-x\leq 0
and
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})<\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})
, or
\theta_{0}-x\geq 0
and
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})>\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})
.

For any
\delta
-fair algorithm, we have that, for any fixed
\theta_{0}
and
x
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}|\theta_{0}}[\mathcal{F}_{t}\text{ is unfair for }(\theta_{0},x)]\leq\delta,

which implies,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{0},\mathcal{F}_{t}}[\mathcal{F}_{t}\text{ is unfair for }(\theta_{0},x)]\leq\delta.

(A.7)

By Lemma
A.3
and (
A.7
), we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t},\theta^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta|\mathcal{F}_{t}}}[\mathcal{F}_{t}\text{ is unfair for }(\theta^{\prime},x)]\leq\delta,

(A.8)

and by Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}\left[\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta|\mathcal{F}_{t}}}[\mathcal{F}_{t}\text{ is unfair for }(\theta^{\prime},x)\geq 6\delta]\right]\leq\frac{1}{6}.

(A.9)

We consider
\mathcal{F}_{t}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{t}
does not
\delta^{\prime}
-distinguish at
x
and
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})>\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})
, then condition on this
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}<

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}}}(\theta^{\prime}\geq x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}}}[\theta^{\prime}\geq x,\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})>\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}}}[\mathcal{F}_{t}\text{ is unfair for }(\theta^{\prime},x)].

(A.10)

Similarly, for
\mathcal{F}_{t}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{t}
does not
\delta^{\prime}
-distinguish at
x
and
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})<\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})
, then condition on this
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}

\displaystyle<\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}}}[\theta^{\prime}\leq x,\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})<\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}}}[\mathcal{F}_{t}\text{ is unfair for }(\theta^{\prime},x)].

(A.11)

Let
\delta^{\prime}=6\delta
. Therefore, by (
A.9
) and (
A.1.3
), the probability of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
that does not
\delta^{\prime}
-distinguish at
x
and
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})>\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})
is less than
\frac{1}{6}
. Hence, with probability at least
\frac{5}{6}
over the marginal distribution of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, it must be that either
\mathcal{F}_{t}

\delta^{\prime}
-distinguishes at
x
, or
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})\leq\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})
. We define this event as
\mathcal{C}_{1}
for notational simplicity. Symmetrically, by (
A.1.3
), with probability at least
\frac{5}{6}
over the marginal distribution of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
,
\mathcal{F}_{t}
either
\delta^{\prime}
-distinguish at
x
or
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})\geq\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})
, where this event is defined as
\mathcal{C}_{2}
for notational simplicity.

Define events
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}=\{s\geq t/16\}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}}=\{|\theta_{0}|\leq 1/4\}
. Since we have proved that, under events
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}}
, together with the conditions
t>8192\log(1/\delta^{\prime})
, and
\delta^{\prime}<1/2
,
\mathcal{F}_{t}

\delta^{\prime}
-distinguishes at
x
implies either
x-\theta_{0}\leq-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}
or
x-\theta_{0}\geq\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}.
Therefore, under events
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, together with the conditions
t>8192\log(1/\delta^{\prime})
, and
\delta^{\prime}<1/2
, we have either
|x-\theta_{0}|\geq\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}
or
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})\leq\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})
; under
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, together with the conditions
t>8192\log(1/\delta^{\prime})
, and
\delta^{\prime}<1/2
, we have either
|x-\theta_{0}|\geq\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}
or
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})\geq\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})
. Putting these events together, it is clear that under
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, together with the conditions
t>8192\log(1/\delta^{\prime})
, and
\delta^{\prime}<1/2
, we have either
|x-\theta_{0}|\geq\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}
or
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})=\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t})=\frac{1}{2}
.

Step 4: Derivation of the Regret Lower Bound

For convenience, we summarize the probability of these events.
For
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
, by its definition, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}(\mathcal{C}_{1})\geq\frac{5}{6},\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}(\mathcal{C}_{2})\geq\frac{5}{6}\implies\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})\geq\frac{4}{6},

which implies, since
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}})=\frac{1}{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}|\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}}}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}})}{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}(\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}})}\geq 2(\frac{4}{6}+\frac{1}{2}-1)\geq\frac{1}{3}.

(A.12)

For any fixed
\theta_{0}
, by (
A.4
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}|\theta_{0}}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}})\geq 1-\delta-\exp\left(-\frac{t}{128}\right).

(A.13)

By (
A.6
), for any fixed
\theta_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}|\theta_{0}}(\mathcal{A}\mid\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}})\geq 1-2\sqrt{4\delta^{\prime}}.

(A.14)

Define
r_{t}:=\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}
. The regret can be bounded from below as

\displaystyle\sup\left\{R_{T}\left(\pi,\pi^{*}\right):\left(P_{X,Y^{(1)}},P_{X,Y^{(2)}}\right)\in\mathcal{P}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left|\theta-x_{t+1}\right|\left[I\left\{x_{t+1}-\theta\geq 0,\pi_{t+1}=2\right\}+I\left\{x_{t+1}-\theta<0,\pi_{t+1}=1\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{0}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta|\mathcal{B}(\theta)}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\theta_{0}-x_{t+1}\right|\left[I\left\{x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}\geq 0,\pi_{t+1}=2\right\}+I\left\{x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}<0,\pi_{t+1}=1\right\}\right]\right].

(A.15)

Let
L_{t+1}=\left|\theta_{0}-x_{t+1}\right|\left[I\left\{x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}\geq 0,\pi_{t+1}=2\right\}+I\left\{x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}<0,\pi_{t+1}=1\right\}\right]
, then

\displaystyle\sup\left\{R_{T}\left(\pi,\pi^{*}\right):\left(P_{X,Y^{(1)}},P_{X,Y^{(2)}}\right)\in\mathcal{P}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{0}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta|\mathcal{B}(\theta)}}\sum_{t=8192\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[L_{t+1}\;\Bigm|\;\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{0}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\theta|\mathcal{B}(\theta)}}\sum_{t=8192\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}^{T-1}I_{1,t+1}\times I_{2,t+1}.

(A.16)

For any
\theta_{0}
drawn from the distribution
\mathbb{P}_{\theta|\mathcal{B}(\theta)}
,
consider
I_{1,t+1}
with
t>8192\log(1/\delta^{\prime})
. Assume
T
is sufficiently large. Let
\delta=\Theta(1/T)
,
\delta^{\prime}={6\delta}=\Theta(1/T)
, then
t
can be chosen as
t=\Theta(\log T)
, and
\delta^{\prime}<1/2
.
According to Step 3, under
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and
|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t}
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=1\mid x_{t+1},\mathcal{F}_{t})=\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t+1}=2\mid x_{t+1},\mathcal{F}_{t})=1/2
. Thus,

\begin{split}I_{1,t+1}&\geq\mathbb{E}\Bigl[|\theta_{0}-x_{t+1}|\bigl(\mathbb{I}\{x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}\geq 0,\pi_{t+1}=2\}\\
&\quad+\mathbb{I}\{x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}<0,\pi_{t+1}=1\}\bigr)\Bigm|\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t}\Bigr]\\
&\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[|\theta_{0}-x_{t+1}|\Bigm|\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t}\Bigr],\end{split}

(A.17)

The events
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
depend on
\mathcal{F}_{t}
(independent of
x_{t+1}
).
It follows that for
\theta_{0}
satisfying
\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}}
, by the independency between these events and
x_{t+1}
, and by (
A.12
), (
A.4
) and (
A.14
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\;\Bigm|\;|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t})=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(1-\delta-\exp(-\frac{t}{128})\right)\left(1-2\sqrt{4\delta^{\prime}}\right)+\frac{1}{3}-1\geq c

for some positive constant
c
, which implies for any
\theta_{0}
satisfying
\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\left|\theta_{0}-x_{t+1}\right|\;\Bigm|\;\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\left|\theta_{0}-x_{t+1}\right|\;\Bigm|\;|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t}\Bigr],

(A.18)

since for fixed
\theta_{0}
, we have
\theta_{0}-x_{t+1}
is independent of
\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\}
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t})>0
.

To compute
\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\left|\theta_{0}-x_{t+1}\right|\Bigm||x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t}\Bigr]
, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\left|\theta_{0}-x_{t+1}\right|I_{|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t}}\Bigr]=\int_{\theta_{0}-r_{t}}^{\theta_{0}+r_{t}}|\theta_{0}-x|\,\cdot\frac{1}{2}\,dx=\frac{1}{2}r_{t}^{2},

together with the fact that the probability
\mathbb{P}(|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t})=r_{t}
, implies

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\left|\theta_{0}-x_{t+1}\right|\;\Bigm|\;|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t}\Bigr]=\frac{r_{t}^{2}/2}{r_{t}}=\frac{r_{t}}{2}.

Therefore, together with (
A.17
) and (
A.1.3
),

I_{1,t+1}\gtrsim\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{r_{t}}{2}=\frac{r_{t}}{4}=\frac{1}{16}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}.

In addition,
I_{2,t+1}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t})\gtrsim\mathbb{P}(|x_{t+1}-\theta_{0}|<r_{t})\gtrsim{r_{t}}\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{\log T}{t}}
(since
r_{t}=\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{2}{\delta^{\prime}}}\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{\log T}{t}}
). The product satisfies
I_{1,t+1}\times I_{2,t+1}\gtrsim\frac{\log T}{t}
. We have for any
\theta_{0}
satisfying
\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=8192\log(1/\delta^{\prime})+128}^{T-1}I_{1,t+1}\times I_{2,t+1}=\Omega((\log T)^{2}),

which, together with (
A.1.3
),(
A.1.3
), leads to

\displaystyle\sup\left\{R_{T}\left(\pi,\pi^{*}\right):\left(P_{X,Y^{(1)}},P_{X,Y^{(2)}}\right)\in\mathcal{P}\right\}\geq\Omega((\log T)^{2}).

\square

A.2
Proofs in Section
3.2

A.2.1
Supporting Lemmas and Propositions

We first present several lemmas and propositions that are used in this section, whose proofs can be found in Section
A.6
. Recall that in Section
3.2
, we take
C=0
in Assumption
8
.

Lemma A.4
.

Under Assumption
8
, if
\max_{i,j\in\mathcal{K}}\max_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\Delta_{i,j}(\bm{x})>T^{-\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}}
, there exists a positive constant
c^{\prime\prime}
such that for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
and
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}\setminus(B(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)})\cup\mathcal{R}_{k})
,
\max_{j\in\mathcal{K}}f_{j}^{*}(\bm{x})-f_{k}^{*}(\bm{x})>c^{\prime\prime}
.

Lemma A.5
.

Under event
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{q-1}
,
\mathcal{R}_{k}\subseteq S_{q,k}
for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
.

Lemma A.6
.

When
T>e^{C_{K}}
,
Q\leq\lceil\frac{\beta}{(2\beta+d)\log 2}\log(T\left(\log T\right)^{-\frac{2\beta+d}{\beta^{\prime}-1}+\frac{2\beta+d}{2\beta}})\rceil
, and for all
1\leq q\leq Q
,
\epsilon_{q}\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta_{A}.

Lemma A.7
.

When
\max_{i,j\in\mathcal{K}}\max_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\Delta_{i,j}(\bm{x})\leq T^{-\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}}
, Under event
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{q-1}\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{q}
,
S_{q,k}=\mathcal{X}
for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
.

Lemma A.8
.

For any
1\leq q\leq Q-1
, and integers
n_{q,k}
that satisfy
n_{q,k}\geq\left(\frac{6\sqrt{M_{\beta}}Lv_{d}p_{\max}}{p^{*}\lambda_{0}\epsilon_{q}}\right)^{\frac{2\beta+d}{\beta}}
, under Assumptions
4
-
6
, we assume that
S_{q,k}
is weakly
(\frac{c_{0}}{2^{d}},H_{q,k})
-regular at all
\bm{x}\in S_{q,k}\cap G
, then for sufficiently large
T
, the estimator
\widehat{f}_{q,k}
based on samples in the
q^{th}
epoch satisfies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\sup_{\bm{x}\in S_{q,k}}\left|\widehat{f}_{q,k}(\bm{x})-f^{*}_{k}(\bm{x})\right|\geq 1/2\epsilon_{q}\mid\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{q-1},\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{q-1},N_{q,k}=n_{q,k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K\delta_{A}^{-d}\left(4+2M_{\beta}^{2}\right)\exp\left(-C_{K}n_{q,k}^{\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+d}}\epsilon_{q}^{2}\right).

where
C_{K}
and
\lambda_{0}
are specified as

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}p_{\text{min }}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}W\in\mathbb{R}^{d},S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|W\|=1\\
S\subset\mathcal{B}(0,1)\text{ is compact, Leb }(S)=c_{0}v_{d}/2^{d}\end{subarray}}\int_{S}\left(\sum_{|s|\leq\mathfrak{b}(\beta)}W_{s}u^{s}\right)^{2}du

\displaystyle C_{K}=

\displaystyle\frac{3p^{*}\lambda_{0}^{2}}{4\left(1+L_{1}\sqrt{d}\right)^{2}}\min\left\{\frac{1}{6KM_{\beta}^{4}p_{\max}v_{d}+2p^{*}\lambda_{0}M_{\beta}^{2}},\frac{1}{54KM_{\beta}v_{d}p_{\max}+6\sqrt{M_{\beta}}p^{*}\lambda_{0}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\frac{1}{54KM_{\beta}L^{2}v_{d}p_{\max}+6\sqrt{M_{\beta}}L\left(Kv_{d}p_{\max}+p^{*}\right)\lambda_{0}}\right\}.

Proposition A.9
.

Fix any positive parameters
\alpha,\beta,d,L,L_{1}
satisfying
\alpha\beta\leq d
. For any admissible policy
\pi
and
T
, there exists a contextual bandit instance satisfying Assumptions
4
-
8
with the provided parameters such that

\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}R_{T}(\pi)=\Omega\left(T^{\frac{\beta+d-\alpha\beta}{2\beta+d}}\right),

where the
\Omega(\cdot)
term only depends on the parameters of the class
\mathcal{P}
and not on
\pi
.

A.2.2
Proof of Proposition
3.4

First we need to clarify the notations used in
31
which is different from ours. In this proof, the two arms are denoted as arm 1 and arm -1 respectively and the epochs are from 1 to
K
. Moreover,
\mathcal{E}_{+1,j}
refers to the exploitation region of arm 1 at epoch
j
, and similarly
\mathcal{E}_{-1,j}
refers to the exploitation region of arm -1 at epoch
j
. The exploitation region of arm
i
means that only arm
i
would be chosen when context falls on this region.

When
t\in\mathcal{T}_{1}
, the algorithm pulls two arms with equal probability, which implies that
\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=1)=\mathbb{P}(\pi_{t}=2)=1/2.
Thus, fairness condition is satisfied trivially. After that, at epoch
k>1
, the algorithm pulls arm
a
when
\bm{x}_{t}\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{a,j}
and randomly pulls arm with equal probability if
\bm{x}_{t}\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{a\in\{-1,+1\}}\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{a,j}\right)
. The fairness condition is obviously satisfied in the latter case.

Then we focus on the scenario that
\bm{x}_{t}\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{a,j}
. By statement(ii) of Lemma 5 in
31
, under certain event
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{k-1}\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
,
\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{a,j}\right)\cap\mathcal{X}\subseteq\{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}:f_{a}^{*}(\bm{x})>f_{-a}^{*}(\bm{x})\}
, which validates the fairness condition. Thus, under certain event
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{k-1}\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}
, the fairness condition is satisfied for epoch
k
by combining both cases. According to Theorem 1 in
31
, if
T
is large enough,
\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{k-1}\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1}\right)\geq 1-\frac{c(k-1)}{T}
where
c
is some positive constant. By taking union bound over all epochs from 2 to
K
, the fairness condition is satisfied with probability
\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{k=2}^{K}(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{k-1}\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{k-1})\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{K-1}\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{K-1})\right)\geq 1-\frac{c(K-1)}{T}=1-\widetilde{O}(1/T)
since Lemma 2 in
31
gives
K=O(\log T)
.
\square

A.2.3
Proof of Proposition
3.5

First, we have that
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(1)}=\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(2)}=\{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}:f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x})=f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x})\}
, thus
\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{x}_{0}}=\{1,2\}=\mathcal{K}
for
\bm{x}_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(1)}=\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(2)}
. If
|f_{1}^{*}(\bm{x})-f_{2}^{*}(\bm{x})|\leq T^{-\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}}+\frac{\sqrt{M_{\beta}}}{\lambda_{0}}CT^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+d}}
for all
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, then Assumption
8
trivially holds. It remains to consider the scenario that there exists an
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
such that
|f_{1}^{*}(\bm{x})-f_{2}^{*}(\bm{x})|>T^{-\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}}+\frac{\sqrt{M_{\beta}}}{\lambda_{0}}CT^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+d}}
. We will show that it satisfies the two conditions in Assumption
8
as follows.

Assumption
8
(i).
We consider two cases.

Case 1,
d=1
.
Since
\beta>1
,
f_{1}^{*\prime}(x)-f^{*\prime}_{2}(x)
is continuous on a compact set
\Omega
, then by the Heine–Cantor theorem, it is uniformly continuous. Then there exists a constant
\mathfrak{r}>0
such that for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)}
and
|x-x_{0}|\leq\mathfrak{r}
,

\displaystyle\left|f_{1}^{*\prime}(x)-f^{*\prime}_{2}(x)-(f_{1}^{*\prime}(x_{0})-f^{*\prime}_{2}(x_{0}))\right|\leq\frac{1}{\widetilde{c}},

which, together with
\left|f_{1}^{*\prime}(x_{0})-f^{*\prime}_{2}(x_{0})\right|>\frac{2}{\widetilde{c}}
, implies that

\displaystyle\left|f_{1}^{*\prime}(x)-f^{*\prime}_{2}(x)\right|\geq\left|f_{1}^{*\prime}(x_{0})-f^{*\prime}_{2}(x_{0})\right|-\frac{1}{\widetilde{c}}>\frac{1}{\widetilde{c}}.

Consider
k=1
without loss of generality. For any
\bm{x}_{1}\in B(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(1)})\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
0\leq\mathfrak{t}\leq 1
, we have
|x_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(x_{1}-x_{0})-x_{0}|\leq\mathfrak{r}
, thus

|f_{1}^{*\prime}(x_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(x_{1}-x_{0}))-f^{*\prime}_{2}(x_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(x_{1}-x_{0}))|>\frac{1}{\widetilde{c}},

which implies

\displaystyle f_{2}^{*}(x_{1})-f_{1}^{*}(x_{1})=\int_{0}^{1}|f_{1}^{*\prime}(x_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(x_{1}-x_{0}))-f^{*\prime}_{2}(x_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(x_{1}-x_{0}))|\times|x_{1}-x_{0}|d\mathfrak{t}\geq\frac{1}{\widetilde{c}}|x_{1}-x_{0}|.

Thus Assumption
8
(i) holds by choosing
\beta^{\prime}=\beta
.

Case 2,
d>1
.
It suffices to consider
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(2)}
because
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(1)}=\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(2)}
in this two-arm scenario. Since
\bm{x}_{0}
is the projection of
\bm{x}_{1}
on
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(2)}
,
\bm{x}_{0}
is the solution of the optimization problem

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(2)}}

\displaystyle\|\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}\|_{2},

s.t.

\displaystyle f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x})-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x})=0.

Since
\beta>1
,
f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x})-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x})
has continuous first-order derivative. Therefore, the Lagrange multiplier theorem implies that there exists a unique Lagrange multiplier
\lambda^{*}
such that

\frac{\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}}{\|\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}\|_{2}}=\lambda^{*}\nabla\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x}_{0})-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x}_{0})\big).

Since we choose
\bm{x}_{1}\in B(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)})\setminus\mathcal{R}^{(k)}
, it can be seen that
\bm{x}_{1}\neq\bm{x}_{0}
, and thus
\lambda^{*}\neq 0
.

Denote
\bm{v}=\frac{(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0})}{\|\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}\|_{2}}
. Therefore,
\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}
is parallel to
\nabla\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x}_{0})-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x}_{0})\big)
, which gives that

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{\bm{v}}\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x}_{0})-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x}_{0})\big)\big|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\frac{\nabla\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x}_{0})-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x}_{0})\big)^{\mathrm{T}}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0})}{\|\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}\|_{2}}\bigg|

\displaystyle=\|\nabla\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x}_{0})-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x}_{0})\big)\|_{2}>\frac{2}{\widetilde{c}}.

Similar to Case 1,
\nabla_{\bm{v}}\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x})-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x})\big)
is continuous for all
\bm{x}=\bm{x}_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0})\in\Omega
, which, together with the Heine–Cantor theorem, implies that
\nabla_{\bm{v}}\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x})-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x})\big)
is uniformly continuous. Therefore, similar to Case 1 again, there exists
\mathfrak{r}>0
such that if
\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{0}\|_{2}=\|\mathfrak{t}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0})\|_{2}\leq\mathfrak{r}
, then
|\nabla_{\bm{v}}\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x})-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x})\big)|>\frac{1}{\widetilde{c}}
. By the fact that
\bm{x}=\bm{x}_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0})
for
0\leq\mathfrak{t}\leq 1
and certainly
\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{0}\|_{2}=\|\mathfrak{t}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0})\|_{2}\leq\mathfrak{r}
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{\bm{v}}\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x}_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}))-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x}_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}))\big)\big|>\frac{1}{\widetilde{c}}

for all
0\leq\mathfrak{t}\leq 1
, which implies, consider
k=1
without loss of generality,

\displaystyle f_{2}^{*}(\bm{x}_{1})-f_{1}^{*}(\bm{x}_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\big|\nabla\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x}_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}))-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x}_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}))\big)\big|^{\mathrm{T}}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0})d\mathfrak{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\big|\nabla_{\bm{v}}\big(f^{*}_{1}(\bm{x}_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}))-f^{*}_{2}(\bm{x}_{0}+\mathfrak{t}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}))\big)\big|d\mathfrak{t}\|\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\widetilde{c}}\|\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0}\|_{2}.

Thus, Assumption
8
(i) holds for the case
d>1
by choosing
\beta^{\prime}=\beta
.

Assumption
8
(ii).
Since
\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{x}_{0}}=\{1,2\}=\mathcal{K}
, there is no arm in
\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{x}_{0}}
, and Assumption
8
(ii) holds trivially. This finishes the proof.
\square

A.2.4
Proof of Proposition
3.6

We prove this proposition by contradiction. Suppose there exists an arm
k
and
\widetilde{\bm{x}}\in S_{q,k}\cap G
such that
S_{q,k}
is not weakly
(\frac{c_{0}}{2^{d}},H_{q,k})
-regular at
\widetilde{\bm{x}}
. Then by Lemma
A.7
, apparently
\max_{i,j\in\mathcal{K}}\max_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\Delta_{i,j}(\bm{x})>T^{-\frac{\beta}{2\beta+d}}
.

First we prove
\widetilde{\bm{x}}\in B(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)})\setminus\mathcal{R}_{k}
, where
B(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)})
is as in Assumption
8
. Assume that
\widetilde{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{k}
. Then
the definition of weakly
(c_{0},r_{0})
-regularity implies that
\mathcal{R}_{k}
is also not weakly
(\frac{c_{0}}{2^{d}},H_{q,k})
-regular at
\widetilde{\bm{x}}
. By Assumption
6
,
\mathcal{R}_{k}
is weakly
(\frac{c_{0}}{2^{d}},H_{q,k})
-regular at all
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{R}_{k}
since
H_{q,k}\leq r_{0}
under event
\mathcal{M}_{q}
and sufficiently large
T
, which leads to a contradiction and implies that
\widetilde{\bm{x}}\notin\mathcal{R}_{k}
. Then it remains to prove
\widetilde{\bm{x}}\in B(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)})
. Since
\widetilde{\bm{x}}\in S_{q,k}
, we have under event
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{q-1}
,

\displaystyle f_{i}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-f_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=f_{i}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-\widehat{f}_{q-1,i}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})+\widehat{f}_{q-1,i}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-\widehat{f}_{q-1,k}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})+\widehat{f}_{q-1,k}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-f_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{q-1}+2(K-1)\epsilon_{q-1}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{q-1}

\displaystyle=(2K-1)\epsilon_{q-1},

where
i=\arg\max_{j\in\mathcal{K}}f_{j}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})
. Thus,
f_{i}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-f_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})\leq(2K-1)\epsilon_{q-1}\leq K(\log T)^{\frac{\beta^{\prime}-1-2\beta}{2\beta^{\prime}-2}}
. By Lemma
A.4
, if
\widetilde{\bm{x}}\notin B(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)})
,
\max_{j\in\mathcal{K}}f_{j}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-f_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})>c^{\prime\prime}
, which contradicts the fact that
f_{i}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-f_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})\leq K(\log T)^{\frac{\beta^{\prime}-1-2\beta}{2\beta^{\prime}-2}}<c^{\prime\prime}
as long as
T
is large enough, since
1<\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta
.

Denote
\bm{x}_{0}
as the projection of
\widetilde{\bm{x}}
on
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)}
. Assumption
8
implies that there exists an arm
j\in{\mathcal{Q}_{\widetilde{\bm{x}}}}
such that
\|\widetilde{\bm{x}}-\bm{x}_{0}\|_{2}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta^{\prime}}}\leq\widetilde{c}(f_{j}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-f_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}}))
. According to Lemma
A.5
, since
j\in{\mathcal{Q}_{\widetilde{\bm{x}}}}
, we have
j\in\mathcal{K}_{q,u(\widetilde{\bm{x}})}
. Moreover, since
\widetilde{\bm{x}}\in S_{q,k}
, which implies
k\in\mathcal{K}_{q,u(\widetilde{\bm{x}})}
, we have
\widehat{f}_{q-1,j}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})
and
\widehat{f}_{q-1,k}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})
are both
(2\epsilon_{q-1})
-chained to
\max_{o\in\mathcal{K}_{q-1,u(\widetilde{\bm{x}})}}\widehat{f}_{q-1,o}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})
. Thus,

\displaystyle f_{j}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-f_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=f_{j}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-\widehat{f}_{q-1,j}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})+\widehat{f}_{q-1,j}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-\widehat{f}_{q-1,k}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})+\widehat{f}_{q-1,k}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})-f_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{q-1}+2(K-1)\epsilon_{q-1}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{q-1}

\displaystyle\leq 2K\epsilon_{q-1},

which implies that
\|\widetilde{\bm{x}}-\bm{x}_{0}\|_{2}\lesssim\epsilon_{q-1}^{\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}}\lesssim 2^{-q\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}}(\log T)^{\frac{(\beta^{\prime}-1-2\beta)\beta^{\prime}}{(2\beta^{\prime}-2)\beta}}
.

Since
H_{q,k}=N_{q,k}^{-1/(2\beta+d)}\geq|{\mathcal{T}}_{q}|^{-1/(2\beta+d)}=\left\lceil\frac{2K}{p^{*}}\left(\frac{4^{q}\log\left(T\delta_{A}^{-d}\right)}{C_{K}}\right)^{\frac{2\beta+d}{2\beta}}\left(\log T\right)^{\frac{2\beta+d}{\beta^{\prime}-1}-\frac{2\beta+d}{2\beta}}+\frac{K^{2}}{2p^{*2}}\log T\right\rceil^{-1/(2\beta+d)}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\|\widetilde{\bm{x}}-\bm{x}_{0}\|_{2}}{H_{q,k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{2^{-q\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}}(\log T)^{\frac{(\beta^{\prime}-1-2\beta)\beta^{\prime}}{(2\beta^{\prime}-2)\beta}}}{\left(\frac{2K}{p^{*}}\left(\frac{4^{q}\log\left(T\delta_{A}^{-d}\right)}{C_{K}}\right)^{\frac{2\beta+d}{2\beta}}\left(\log T\right)^{\frac{2\beta+d}{\beta^{\prime}-1}-\frac{2\beta+d}{2\beta}}+\frac{K^{2}}{2p^{*2}}\log T\right)^{-1/(2\beta+d)}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\log T)^{\frac{\beta^{\prime}-2\beta}{2\beta}}2^{q(\frac{1-\beta^{\prime}}{\beta})}\leq 1-(1-\frac{c_{0}}{2^{d}})^{\frac{1}{d}},

(A.19)

where the last inequality is by taking
T
large enough and
